Is that… is that the kind of frequency you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other ways you try to compensate for the calories you consume during these evening snacking episodes, besides making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, besides that, I can’t think of anything specific I do. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, I guess I’ve been having some trouble with eating, mostly in the evenings. It’s not every night or anything, but after my evening class I’ll sometimes stop and get a full fast-food meal, and then maybe candy from the vending machine, and when I get home I’ll keep snacking, like I’m trying to make up for not eating much during the day. It feels a little embarrassing to say out loud, but… that’s the main reason I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but I do feel pretty bad afterward. Once I start on the snacks, I’ll tell myself, okay, close the bag and just go to bed, but then my hand keeps going back for more. And by the end I just feel embarrassed, kind of sick of myself, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often restrict your food intake during the day before these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it restricting, exactly. I mean, I do sometimes keep things pretty light during the day, but I don’t know if that’s on purpose or just being busy and not wanting to think about food at work. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you intentionally limit how much you eat during the day?\n\nPatient: I guess… not in a very planned-out way, if that makes sense. I don’t sit there with rules written down or anything. I’ll just think, maybe I should keep it light today, or I’m busy and I’ll have coffee instead of a real lunch. So… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing it on purpose every time. Is that the kind of answer you’re looking for?\n\nDoctor: How often do these evening snacking episodes happen where you feel like you're losing control and eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s not every night or anything. But I guess for the last three months or so, it’s usually happened maybe once a week—often after class—where I eat more than I meant to and feel like I can’t really stop. And… I’m embarrassed to say this, but afterward I’ve made myself throw up. Is that… is that the kind of frequency you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other ways you try to compensate for the calories you consume during these evening snacking episodes, besides making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, besides that, I can’t think of anything specific I do. I might tell myself I’ll be “better” the next day, but I don’t have some other thing I’m doing to make up for it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. My clothes seem about the same, and I haven’t really been tracking the number closely. Maybe it goes up or down a little, but I haven’t noticed a big change. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the right way to put it. I tell myself, “Okay, just close the bag and go to bed,” and I really mean to, but then I just keep reaching for more. It’s like I can’t quite stop myself in the moment, and afterward I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say really quickly… I mean, I’m not sitting there timing it or anything. It can feel a little mindless, like I’m just snacking without paying much attention, but I don’t know that I’d call it unusually fast. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight when you're eating during these episodes or afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but yes. Especially afterward. If I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach. Then I start thinking people in class are going to look at me like I’m some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. So… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing it on purpose every time. Is that the kind of answer you’re looking for?\n\nDoctor: How often do these evening snacking episodes happen where you feel like you're losing control and eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s not every night or anything. But I guess for the last three months or so, it’s usually happened maybe once a week—often after class—where I eat more than I meant to and feel like I can’t really stop. And… I’m embarrassed to say this, but afterward I’ve made myself throw up. Is that… is that the kind of frequency you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other ways you try to compensate for the calories you consume during these evening snacking episodes, besides making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, besides that, I can’t think of anything specific I do. I might tell myself I’ll be “better” the next day, but I don’t have some other thing I’m doing to make up for it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. My clothes seem about the same, and I haven’t really been tracking the number closely. Maybe it goes up or down a little, but I haven’t noticed a big change. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the right way to put it. I tell myself, “Okay, just close the bag and go to bed,” and I really mean to, but then I just keep reaching for more. It’s like I can’t quite stop myself in the moment, and afterward I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say really quickly… I mean, I’m not sitting there timing it or anything. It can feel a little mindless, like I’m just snacking without paying much attention, but I don’t know that I’d call it unusually fast. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight when you're eating during these episodes or afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but yes. Especially afterward. If I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach. Then I start thinking people in class are going to look at me like I’m some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. I know that probably sounds a little vain or dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about gaining weight what drives you to restrict your food intake during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m doing anything extreme, but when the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I get this knot in my stomach. Then I start thinking people in class are going to see me as some sloppy old guy who let himself go. So during the day I do try to be more careful, I guess, to keep that from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically engage in these evening snacking episodes when you are alone?\n\nPatient: I guess… sometimes, yes, but I’m not sure it’s always that deliberate. It’s usually just when things are quiet in the evening, so I might be by myself. I haven’t really tracked it that closely, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern after these episodes the weight you might have gained?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, but if I see the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I get this tight knot in my stomach. Then my mind goes to, you know, people in class noticing and thinking I’m just some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. Then my mind goes to, you know, people in class noticing and thinking I’m just some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. I know that may not be realistic, but that’s what I worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your evening snacking episodes are more about eating a large amount of food in a short time or feeling like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the not being able to stop, if I’m being honest. But I guess for the last three months or so, it’s usually happened maybe once a week—often after class—where I eat more than I meant to and feel like I can’t really stop. And… I’m embarrassed to say this, but afterward I’ve made myself throw up. Is that… is that the kind of frequency you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other ways you try to compensate for the calories you consume during these evening snacking episodes, besides making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, besides that, I can’t think of anything specific I do. I might tell myself I’ll be “better” the next day, but I don’t have some other thing I’m doing to make up for it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. My clothes seem about the same, and I haven’t really been tracking the number closely. Maybe it goes up or down a little, but I haven’t noticed a big change. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the right way to put it. I tell myself, “Okay, just close the bag and go to bed,” and I really mean to, but then I just keep reaching for more. It’s like I can’t quite stop myself in the moment, and afterward I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say really quickly… I mean, I’m not sitting there timing it or anything. It can feel a little mindless, like I’m just snacking without paying much attention, but I don’t know that I’d call it unusually fast. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight when you're eating during these episodes or afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but yes. Especially afterward. If I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach. Then I start thinking people in class are going to look at me like I’m some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. I know that probably sounds a little vain or dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about gaining weight what drives you to restrict your food intake during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m doing anything extreme, but when the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I get this knot in my stomach. Then I start thinking people in class are going to see me as some sloppy old guy who let himself go. So during the day I do try to be more careful, I guess, to keep that from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically engage in these evening snacking episodes when you are alone?\n\nPatient: I guess… sometimes, yes, but I’m not sure it’s always that deliberate. It’s usually just when things are quiet in the evening, so I might be by myself. I haven’t really tracked it that closely, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern after these episodes the weight you might have gained?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, but if I see the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I get this tight knot in my stomach. Then my mind goes to, you know, people in class noticing and thinking I’m just some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. I know that may not be realistic, but that’s what I worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your evening snacking episodes are more about eating a large amount of food in a short time or feeling like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the not being able to stop, if I’m being honest. I mean, it’s not like I plan to eat some huge amount, but once I start on the snacks, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just close the bag and go to bed.” And then my hand just keeps going back for more. Afterward I feel pretty embarrassed… kind of sick of myself, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the loss of control during your evening snacking episodes is more related to the amount of food you're eating or the feeling that you cannot stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: It’s more the feeling that I can’t stop, I think. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but once I start on those snacks, I’ll keep telling myself, “Just close the bag and go to bed,” and then my hand keeps reaching back in anyway. Afterward I feel embarrassed… kind of sick of myself. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight when you're eating during these episodes or afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but yes. Especially afterward. If I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach. Then I start thinking people in class are going to look at me like I’m some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. I know that probably sounds a little vain or dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about gaining weight what drives you to restrict your food intake during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m doing anything extreme, but when the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I get this knot in my stomach. Then I start thinking people in class are going to see me as some sloppy old guy who let himself go. So during the day I do try to be more careful, I guess, to keep that from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically engage in these evening snacking episodes when you are alone?\n\nPatient: I guess… sometimes, yes, but I’m not sure it’s always that deliberate. It’s usually just when things are quiet in the evening, so I might be by myself. I haven’t really tracked it that closely, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern after these episodes the weight you might have gained?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, but if I see the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I get this tight knot in my stomach. Then my mind goes to, you know, people in class noticing and thinking I’m just some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. I know that may not be realistic, but that’s what I worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your evening snacking episodes are more about eating a large amount of food in a short time or feeling like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the not being able to stop, if I’m being honest. I mean, it’s not like I plan to eat some huge amount, but once I start on the snacks, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just close the bag and go to bed.” And then my hand just keeps going back for more. Afterward I feel pretty embarrassed… kind of sick of myself, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the loss of control during your evening snacking episodes is more related to the amount of food you're eating or the feeling that you cannot stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: It’s more the feeling that I can’t stop, I think. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but once I start on those snacks, I’ll keep telling myself, “Just close the bag and go to bed,” and then my hand keeps reaching back in anyway. Afterward I feel embarrassed… kind of sick of myself. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the loss of control and eating during these episodes is associated with eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is a large amount, when I actually say it out loud. I don’t like admitting that. After my evening class, I’ll sometimes stop and get a full fast-food meal, and then maybe candy from the vending machine, and then when I get home I keep going with snacks. It feels like I’m making up for the whole day or something. I know that probably sounds bad.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, I guess I’ve been having some trouble with eating, mostly in the evenings. It’s not every night or anything, but after my evening class I’ll sometimes stop and get a full fast-food meal, and then maybe candy from the vending machine, and when I get home I’ll keep snacking, like I’m trying to make up for not eating much during the day. It feels a little embarrassing to say out loud, but… that’s the main reason I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but I do feel pretty bad afterward. Once I start on the snacks, I’ll tell myself, okay, close the bag and just go to bed, but then my hand keeps going back for more. And by the end I just feel embarrassed, kind of sick of myself, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often restrict your food intake during the day before these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it restricting, exactly. I mean, I do sometimes keep things pretty light during the day, but I don’t know if that’s on purpose or just being busy and not wanting to think about food at work. I don’t sit there with rules written down or anything. I’ll just think, maybe I should keep it light today, or I’m busy and I’ll have coffee instead of a real lunch. Then I start thinking people in class are going to see me as some sloppy old guy who let himself go. So during the day I do try to be more careful, I guess, to keep that from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically engage in these evening snacking episodes when you are alone?\n\nPatient: I guess… sometimes, yes, but I’m not sure it’s always that deliberate. It’s usually just when things are quiet in the evening, so I might be by myself. I haven’t really tracked it that closely, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern after these episodes the weight you might have gained?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, but if I see the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I get this tight knot in my stomach. Then my mind goes to, you know, people in class noticing and thinking I’m just some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. I know that may not be realistic, but that’s what I worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your evening snacking episodes are more about eating a large amount of food in a short time or feeling like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the not being able to stop, if I’m being honest. I mean, it’s not like I plan to eat some huge amount, but once I start on the snacks, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just close the bag and go to bed.” And then my hand just keeps going back for more. Afterward I feel pretty embarrassed… kind of sick of myself, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the loss of control during your evening snacking episodes is more related to the amount of food you're eating or the feeling that you cannot stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: It’s more the feeling that I can’t stop, I think. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but once I start on those snacks, I’ll keep telling myself, “Just close the bag and go to bed,” and then my hand keeps reaching back in anyway. Afterward I feel embarrassed… kind of sick of myself. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the loss of control and eating during these episodes is associated with eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is a large amount, when I actually say it out loud. I don’t like admitting that. After my evening class, I’ll sometimes stop and get a full fast-food meal, and then maybe candy from the vending machine, and then when I get home I keep going with snacks. It feels like I’m making up for the whole day or something. I know that probably sounds bad.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating where you feel like you can't stop happened at least once a week for the past three months consistently, or has the frequency varied?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been pretty consistent… I mean, I don’t want to exaggerate it, but for about the last three months there’s usually been at least one night a week after class where I overeat like that. And… afterward I make myself throw up. I’m sorry, that’s hard to say, but yes, about once a week.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n18\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF50.8, F50.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, I guess I’ve been having some trouble with eating, mostly in the evenings. It’s not every night or anything, but after my evening class I’ll sometimes stop and get a full fast-food meal, and then maybe candy from the vending machine, and when I get home I’ll keep snacking, like I’m trying to make up for not eating much during the day. It feels a little embarrassing to say out loud, but… that’s the main reason I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but I do feel pretty bad afterward. Once I start on the snacks, I’ll tell myself, okay, close the bag and just go to bed, but then my hand keeps going back for more. And by the end I just feel embarrassed, kind of sick of myself, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often restrict your food intake during the day before these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it restricting, exactly. I mean, I do sometimes keep things pretty light during the day, but I don’t know if that’s on purpose or just being busy and not wanting to think about food at work. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you intentionally limit how much you eat during the day?\n\nPatient: I guess… not in a very planned-out way, if that makes sense. I don’t sit there with rules written down or anything. I’ll just think, maybe I should keep it light today, or I’m busy and I’ll have coffee instead of a real lunch. So… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing it on purpose every time. Is that the kind of answer you’re looking for?\n\nDoctor: How often do these evening snacking episodes happen where you feel like you're losing control and eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s not every night or anything. But I guess for the last three months or so, it’s usually happened maybe once a week—often after class—where I eat more than I meant to and feel like I can’t really stop. And… I’m embarrassed to say this, but afterward I’ve made myself throw up. Is that… is that the kind of frequency you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other ways you try to compensate for the calories you consume during these evening snacking episodes, besides making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, besides that, I can’t think of anything specific I do. I might tell myself I’ll be “better” the next day, but I don’t have some other thing I’m doing to make up for it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. My clothes seem about the same, and I haven’t really been tracking the number closely. Maybe it goes up or down a little, but I haven’t noticed a big change. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the right way to put it. I tell myself, “Okay, just close the bag and go to bed,” and I really mean to, but then I just keep reaching for more. It’s like I can’t quite stop myself in the moment, and afterward I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say really quickly… I mean, I’m not sitting there timing it or anything. It can feel a little mindless, like I’m just snacking without paying much attention, but I don’t know that I’d call it unusually fast. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight when you're eating during these episodes or afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but yes. It’s usually just when things are quiet in the evening, so I might be by myself. I haven’t really tracked it that closely, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern after these episodes the weight you might have gained?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, but if I see the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I get this tight knot in my stomach. Then my mind goes to, you know, people in class noticing and thinking I’m just some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. I know that may not be realistic, but that’s what I worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your evening snacking episodes are more about eating a large amount of food in a short time or feeling like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the not being able to stop, if I’m being honest. I mean, it’s not like I plan to eat some huge amount, but once I start on the snacks, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just close the bag and go to bed.” And then my hand just keeps going back for more. Afterward I feel pretty embarrassed… kind of sick of myself, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the loss of control during your evening snacking episodes is more related to the amount of food you're eating or the feeling that you cannot stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: It’s more the feeling that I can’t stop, I think. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but once I start on those snacks, I’ll keep telling myself, “Just close the bag and go to bed,” and then my hand keeps reaching back in anyway. Afterward I feel embarrassed… kind of sick of myself. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the loss of control and eating during these episodes is associated with eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is a large amount, when I actually say it out loud. I don’t like admitting that. After my evening class, I’ll sometimes stop and get a full fast-food meal, and then maybe candy from the vending machine, and then when I get home I keep going with snacks. It feels like I’m making up for the whole day or something. I know that probably sounds bad.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating where you feel like you can't stop happened at least once a week for the past three months consistently, or has the frequency varied?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been pretty consistent… I mean, I don’t want to exaggerate it, but for about the last three months there’s usually been at least one night a week after class where I overeat like that. And… afterward I make myself throw up. I’m sorry, that’s hard to say, but yes, about once a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in response to emotions or stress during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat,” exactly. It’s more like the evening comes around and I just sort of… start doing it. Maybe stress is part of it, but I haven’t really paid close attention to that. Does that make sense?\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um… well, I guess I’ve been having some trouble with eating, mostly in the evenings. It’s not every night or anything, but after my evening class I’ll sometimes stop and get a full fast-food meal, and then maybe candy from the vending machine, and when I get home I’ll keep snacking, like I’m trying to make up for not eating much during the day. It feels a little embarrassing to say out loud, but… that’s the main reason I thought I should come in.\n\nDoctor: Do you usually feel guilty after these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal of it, but I do feel pretty bad afterward. Once I start on the snacks, I’ll tell myself, okay, close the bag and just go to bed, but then my hand keeps going back for more. And by the end I just feel embarrassed, kind of sick of myself, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often restrict your food intake during the day before these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure I’d call it restricting, exactly. I mean, I do sometimes keep things pretty light during the day, but I don’t know if that’s on purpose or just being busy and not wanting to think about food at work. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you intentionally limit how much you eat during the day?\n\nPatient: I guess… not in a very planned-out way, if that makes sense. I don’t sit there with rules written down or anything. I’ll just think, maybe I should keep it light today, or I’m busy and I’ll have coffee instead of a real lunch. So… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing it on purpose every time. Is that the kind of answer you’re looking for?\n\nDoctor: How often do these evening snacking episodes happen where you feel like you're losing control and eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s not every night or anything. But I guess for the last three months or so, it’s usually happened maybe once a week—often after class—where I eat more than I meant to and feel like I can’t really stop. And… I’m embarrassed to say this, but afterward I’ve made myself throw up. Is that… is that the kind of frequency you mean?\n\nDoctor: Are there any other ways you try to compensate for the calories you consume during these evening snacking episodes, besides making yourself throw up?\n\nPatient: Not really, no… I mean, besides that, I can’t think of anything specific I do. I might tell myself I’ll be “better” the next day, but I don’t have some other thing I’m doing to make up for it. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant changes in your weight over the past few months?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not anything I’d call significant. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the right way to put it. It’s like I can’t quite stop myself in the moment, and afterward I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say really quickly… I mean, I’m not sitting there timing it or anything. It can feel a little mindless, like I’m just snacking without paying much attention, but I don’t know that I’d call it unusually fast. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight when you're eating during these episodes or afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but yes. I know that probably sounds a little vain or dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about gaining weight what drives you to restrict your food intake during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m doing anything extreme, but when the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I get this knot in my stomach. Then I start thinking people in class are going to see me as some sloppy old guy who let himself go. So during the day I do try to be more careful, I guess, to keep that from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically engage in these evening snacking episodes when you are alone?\n\nPatient: I guess… sometimes, yes, but I’m not sure it’s always that deliberate. It’s usually just when things are quiet in the evening, so I might be by myself. I haven’t really tracked it that closely, though. Is that the kind of thing you mean?\n\nDoctor: Is your main concern after these episodes the weight you might have gained?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think that’s probably the main thing, honestly. I don’t want to overstate it, but if I see the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I get this tight knot in my stomach. Then my mind goes to, you know, people in class noticing and thinking I’m just some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. I know that may not be realistic, but that’s what I worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your evening snacking episodes are more about eating a large amount of food in a short time or feeling like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the not being able to stop, if I’m being honest. I mean, it’s not like I plan to eat some huge amount, but once I start on the snacks, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just close the bag and go to bed.” And then my hand just keeps going back for more. Afterward I feel pretty embarrassed… kind of sick of myself, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the loss of control during your evening snacking episodes is more related to the amount of food you're eating or the feeling that you cannot stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: It’s more the feeling that I can’t stop, I think. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but once I start on those snacks, I’ll keep telling myself, “Just close the bag and go to bed,” and then my hand keeps reaching back in anyway. Afterward I feel embarrassed… kind of sick of myself. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the loss of control and eating during these episodes is associated with eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is a large amount, when I actually say it out loud. I don’t like admitting that. After my evening class, I’ll sometimes stop and get a full fast-food meal, and then maybe candy from the vending machine, and then when I get home I keep going with snacks. It feels like I’m making up for the whole day or something. I know that probably sounds bad.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating where you feel like you can't stop happened at least once a week for the past three months consistently, or has the frequency varied?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been pretty consistent… I mean, I don’t want to exaggerate it, but for about the last three months there’s usually been at least one night a week after class where I overeat like that. And… afterward I make myself throw up. I’m sorry, that’s hard to say, but yes, about once a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in response to emotions or stress during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. I mean, I wouldn’t say I’m sitting there thinking, “I’m upset, so I’m going to eat,” exactly. It’s more like the evening comes around and I just sort of… start doing it. Maybe stress is part of it, but I haven’t really paid close attention to that. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the amount of food you eat during these episodes is larger than what most people would eat under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying it that way, but when I stop after my evening class, it can be a full fast-food meal, then candy from the vending machine, and then I still eat more snacks once I’m home. So… compared to what most people would probably eat in that situation, yeah, it’s more than normal, I guess.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I don’t sit there with rules written down or anything. I’ll just think, maybe I should keep it light today, or I’m busy and I’ll have coffee instead of a real lunch. So… maybe sometimes, but I’m not sure I’d say I’m doing it on purpose every time. Is that the kind of answer you’re looking for?\n\nDoctor: How often do these evening snacking episodes happen where you feel like you're losing control and eating more than you intended?\n\nPatient: Um… it’s not every night or anything. But I guess for the last three months or so, it’s usually happened maybe once a week—often after class—where I eat more than I meant to and feel like I can’t really stop. And… I’m embarrassed to say this, but afterward I’ve made myself throw up. I might tell myself I’ll be “better” the next day, but I don’t have some other thing I’m doing to make up for it. My clothes seem about the same, and I haven’t really been tracking the number closely. Maybe it goes up or down a little, but I haven’t noticed a big change. Is that helpful?\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're unable to stop eating or control what you're eating during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess that’s probably the right way to put it. I tell myself, “Okay, just close the bag and go to bed,” and I really mean to, but then I just keep reaching for more. It’s like I can’t quite stop myself in the moment, and afterward I feel embarrassed and kind of sick of myself.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating really quickly during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d say really quickly… I mean, I’m not sitting there timing it or anything. It can feel a little mindless, like I’m just snacking without paying much attention, but I don’t know that I’d call it unusually fast. Does that answer what you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Are you worried about gaining weight when you're eating during these episodes or afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I try not to make a big deal out of it, but yes. Especially afterward. If I get on the scale the next morning and it’s even up a pound, I get this knot in my stomach. Then I start thinking people in class are going to look at me like I’m some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. I know that probably sounds a little vain or dramatic, but that’s where my mind goes.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about gaining weight what drives you to restrict your food intake during the day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it is, at least partly. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m doing anything extreme, but when the scale is up even a pound the next morning, I get this knot in my stomach. Then I start thinking people in class are going to see me as some sloppy old guy who let himself go. So during the day I do try to be more careful, I guess, to keep that from happening.\n\nDoctor: Do you typically engage in these evening snacking episodes when you are alone?\n\nPatient: I guess… sometimes, yes, but I’m not sure it’s always that deliberate. It’s usually just when things are quiet in the evening, so I might be by myself. I haven’t really tracked it that closely, though. Then my mind goes to, you know, people in class noticing and thinking I’m just some sloppy old guy who’s let himself go. I know that may not be realistic, but that’s what I worry about.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your evening snacking episodes are more about eating a large amount of food in a short time or feeling like you can't stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: I think it’s more the not being able to stop, if I’m being honest. I mean, it’s not like I plan to eat some huge amount, but once I start on the snacks, I’ll tell myself, “Okay, just close the bag and go to bed.” And then my hand just keeps going back for more. Afterward I feel pretty embarrassed… kind of sick of myself, I guess. Is that what you mean?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the loss of control during your evening snacking episodes is more related to the amount of food you're eating or the feeling that you cannot stop eating once you've started?\n\nPatient: It’s more the feeling that I can’t stop, I think. I don’t want to make it sound worse than it is, but once I start on those snacks, I’ll keep telling myself, “Just close the bag and go to bed,” and then my hand keeps reaching back in anyway. Afterward I feel embarrassed… kind of sick of myself. Is that the kind of thing you’re asking?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the loss of control and eating during these episodes is associated with eating a large amount of food?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I guess it is a large amount, when I actually say it out loud. I don’t like admitting that. After my evening class, I’ll sometimes stop and get a full fast-food meal, and then maybe candy from the vending machine, and then when I get home I keep going with snacks. It feels like I’m making up for the whole day or something. I know that probably sounds bad.\n\nDoctor: Have these episodes of eating where you feel like you can't stop happened at least once a week for the past three months consistently, or has the frequency varied?\n\nPatient: I think it’s been pretty consistent… I mean, I don’t want to exaggerate it, but for about the last three months there’s usually been at least one night a week after class where I overeat like that. And… afterward I make myself throw up. I’m sorry, that’s hard to say, but yes, about once a week.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you're eating in response to emotions or stress during these evening snacking episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure. Maybe stress is part of it, but I haven’t really paid close attention to that. Does that make sense?\n\nDoctor: Do you think the amount of food you eat during these episodes is larger than what most people would eat under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I don’t like saying it that way, but when I stop after my evening class, it can be a full fast-food meal, then candy from the vending machine, and then I still eat more snacks once I’m home. So… compared to what most people would probably eat in that situation, yeah, it’s more than normal, I guess.\n\nDoctor: Do you make yourself throw up after every episode of eating where you feel like you can't stop?\n\nPatient: Not every time, no. I mean… a few times after those late-night eating spells, I’ve gone to the bathroom and made myself throw up. I get scared that all that food is going to show on me. I know that probably sounds bad… but it’s not after every single time."
